# Milky Way

This article is about the galaxy. For other uses, see [Milky Way (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milky_Way_\(disambiguation\)).

Artist's impression of the structure of the Milky Way based on data from the [European Space Agency](10363.md)'s [Gaia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaia_\(spacecraft\)) telescope, including the location of the spiral arms, bar, and bulge

The **Milky Way** or **Milky Way Galaxy**,[^cite_note-context-30] or simply the **Galaxy**,[^cite_note-poggio-31] is the [galaxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galaxy) that includes the [Solar System](26903.md), with the name describing the [galaxy's appearance](#Appearance) from [Earth](9228.md): a hazy band of light seen in the [night sky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Night_sky) formed from stars in other arms of the galaxy, which are so far away that they cannot be individually distinguished by the [naked eye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naked_eye).

The Milky Way is a [barred spiral galaxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barred_spiral_galaxy) with a [D<sub>25</sub> isophotal diameter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galaxy#Isophotal_diameter) estimated at 26.8 ± 1.1 [kiloparsecs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parsec#Parsecs_and_kiloparsecs) (87,400 ± 3,600 [light-years](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light-year)),[^cite_note-Goodwin-13] but only about 1,000 [light-years](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light-year) thick at the spiral arms (more at the bar). Recent simulations suggest that a [dark matter](8651.md) area, also containing some visible stars, may extend up to a diameter of almost 2 million light-years (613 kpc).[^cite_note-croswell2020-32][^cite_note-dearson2020-33] The Milky Way has several [satellite galaxies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Milky_Way's_satellite_galaxies) and is part of the [Local Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_Group) of galaxies, forming part of the [Virgo Supercluster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virgo_Supercluster) which is itself a component of the [Laniakea Supercluster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laniakea_Supercluster).[^cite_note-34][^cite_note-ARX20140902-35]

It is estimated to contain 100–400 billion stars[^cite_note-36][^cite_note-37] and at least that number of [planets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planet).[^cite_note-Nature-20120111-38][^cite_note-Space-20130102-39] The Solar System is located at a radius of about 27,000 light-years (8.3 kpc) from the [Galactic Center](373352.md),[^cite_note-Gillessen2016-40] on the inner edge of the [Orion Arm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orion_Arm), one of the spiral-shaped concentrations of gas and dust. The stars in the innermost 10,000 light-years form a [bulge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulge_\(astronomy\)) and one or more bars that radiate from the bulge. The Galactic Center is an intense radio source known as [Sagittarius A\*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sagittarius_A*), a [supermassive black hole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supermassive_black_hole) with a mass of 4.100 (± 0.034) million [solar masses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_mass).[^cite_note-NYT-20220131-41][^cite_note-ARX-20220128-42] The oldest stars in the Milky Way are nearly as old as the [universe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universe) itself and thus probably formed shortly after the [Dark Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chronology_of_the_universe#Dark_Ages) of the [Big Bang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Bang).[^cite_note-HD_140283_(arXiv)-43]

[Galileo Galilei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galileo_Galilei) first resolved the band of light into individual stars with his telescope in 1610. Until the early 1920s, most astronomers thought that the Milky Way contained all the stars in the universe.[^cite_note-44] Following the 1920 [Great Debate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Debate_\(astronomy\)) between the astronomers [Harlow Shapley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harlow_Shapley) and [Heber Doust Curtis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heber_Doust_Curtis),[^cite_note-shapley_curtis-45] observations by [Edwin Hubble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edwin_Hubble) in 1923 showed that the Milky Way was just one of many galaxies.

## Mythology

Main article: [Milky Way in mythology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milky_Way_in_mythology)

In the [Babylonian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babylonia) epic poem *[Enūma Eliš](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/En%C5%ABma_Eli%C5%A1)*, the Milky Way is created from the severed tail of the primeval salt water [dragon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dragon) [Tiamat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiamat), set in the sky by [Marduk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marduk), the Babylonian [national god](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_god), after slaying her.[^cite_note-46][^cite_note-47] This story was once thought to have been based on an older [Sumerian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sumer) version in which Tiamat is instead slain by [Enlil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enlil) of [Nippur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nippur),[^cite_note-48][^cite_note-Lambert1964-49] but is now thought to be purely an invention of Babylonian propagandists with the intention of showing Marduk as superior to the Sumerian deities.[^cite_note-Lambert1964-49]

## Etymology

In [Greek mythology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_mythology), [Zeus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zeus) places [Heracles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heracles), his infant son born to [Alcmene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alcmene), on [Hera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hera)'s breast while she is asleep so the baby will drink [her divine milk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milk_of_Hera) and become immortal. Hera wakes up while breastfeeding and then realizes she is nursing an unknown baby: she pushes the baby away, some of her milk spills, and it produces the band of light known as the Milky Way.[^cite_note-waller_hodge2003-50] In another Greek story, the abandoned Heracles is given by [Athena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athena) to Hera for feeding, but Heracles' forcefulness causes Hera to rip him from her breast in pain.[^cite_note-51][^cite_note-Leeming-52][^cite_note-Pache-53]

In Western culture, the name "Milky Way" derives from its appearance as a dim, unresolved, "milky" glowing band arching across the night sky. The term is a translation of the [Classical Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Latin) *via lactea*, in turn derived from the [Hellenistic Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenistic_Greek) γαλαξίας, short for γαλαξίας κύκλος (*_galaxías kýklos_*), meaning "milky circle". The [Ancient Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek) γαλαξίας (*_galaxias_*) – from root γαλακτ-, γάλα ("milk") + -ίας (forming adjectives) – is also the root of "galaxy", the name for our, and later all such, collections of stars.[^cite_note-eo_galaxy-54][^cite_note-jankowski2010-55][^cite_note-oxford-56] The Milky Way, or "milk circle", was just one of 11 "circles" the Greeks identified in the sky, others being the [zodiac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zodiac), the [meridian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meridian_\(astronomy\)), the [horizon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horizon), the [equator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equator), the [tropics of Cancer and Capricorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropics_of_Cancer_and_Capricorn), the [Arctic Circle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arctic_Circle) and the [Antarctic Circle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antarctic_Circle), and two [colure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colure) circles passing through both poles.[^cite_note-eratosthenes1997-57] The English term can be traced back to a story by [Geoffrey Chaucer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geoffrey_Chaucer) c. 1380:

> See yonder, lo, the Galaxyë\
> &#x20;Which men [clepeth](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/clepe) *the Milky Wey*,\
> &#x20;For hit is whyt: and somme, parfey,

— *[The House of Fame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_House_of_Fame)*[^cite_note-eo_galaxy-54]

### Other common names

- "Birds' Path" is used in several [Uralic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uralic_languages) and [Turkic languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_languages) and in the [Baltic languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_languages). Northern peoples observed that [migratory birds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Migratory_birds) follow the course of the galaxy

  [^cite_note-58]

  &#x20;while migrating in the Northern Hemisphere. The name "Birds' Path" (in Finnish, Estonian, Latvian, Lithuanian, Bashkir, and Kazakh) has some variations in other languages, e.g., "Way of the grey (wild) goose" in Chuvash, Mari, and Tatar, and "Way of the Crane" in Erzya and Moksha.

- The [Kaurna people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaurna_people) of the [Adelaide Plains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adelaide_Plains) of South Australia called the Milky Way *wodliparri* in the [Kaurna language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaurna_language), meaning "house river".

  [^cite_note-59]

- The [Gomeroi people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamilaraay) between [New South Wales](21654.md) and [Queensland](59051.md) called the Milky Way *Dhinawan*, the giant "[Emu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emu) in the Sky" that it stretches across the night sky.

  [^cite_note-60]

- The Milky Way was traditionally used as a guide by [pilgrims](23763.md) traveling to the holy site at [Santiago de Compostela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santiago_de_Compostela), hence the use of "The Road to Santiago" as a name for the Milky Way.

  [^cite_note-Fiona-61]

  &#x20;Curiously, *La Voje Ladee* ("The Milky Way") was also used to refer to the pilgrimage road.

  [^cite_note-62]

- River Ganga of the Sky: this Sanskrit name (आकाशगंगा *Ākāśagaṃgā*) is used in many Indian languages following a Hindu belief.

- The Chinese name "Silver River" (銀河) is used throughout East Asia, including Korea and Vietnam (*Ngân hà*). In Japan and Korea, "Silver River" ([Japanese](15606.md): 銀河, [romanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Japanese): *ginga*; [Korean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_language): 은하; [RR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revised_Romanization_of_Korean): *eunha*) refers to any galaxy.

- The Japanese name for the Milky Way is the "River of Heaven" (天の川, *Ama no gawa*), as well as an alternative name in Chinese ([Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_language): 天河; [pinyin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinyin): *Tiānhé*). In Vietnamese, "River of Heaven" (*Thiên hà*) refers to any galaxy.

- In West Asia, Central Asia, and parts of the Balkans, the name for the Milky Way is related to the word for *straw*. Today, Persians, Pakistanis, and Turks use it alongside Arabs, known to them as Darb at-Tabbānah ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic): درب التبانة) meaning "Pathway of the Straw". It has been suggested that the term was spread by medieval [Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab) who in turn borrowed it from Armenians.

  [^cite_note-63]

- In [Serbo-Croatian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbo-Croatian) it is interchangeably called "Kumova slama" (*lit. transl.* Godfather's Straw) along the "Mliječni put" (*lit. transl.* Milky Way).

  [^cite_note-64]

- Scandinavian peoples, such as Swedes, have called the galaxy "Winter Street" (*Vintergatan*) as the galaxy is most clearly visible during the winter in the northern hemisphere, especially at high latitudes where the [glow of the Sun late at night](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midnight_sun) can obscure it during the summer.

## Appearance



The Milky Way as seen from a dark site with little [light pollution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_pollution)

The Milky Way is visible as a hazy band of white light, some 30° wide, arching in the [night sky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Night_sky).[^cite_note-pasachoff1994-65] Although all the individual naked-eye stars in the entire sky are part of the Milky Way Galaxy, the term "Milky Way" is limited to this band of light.[^cite_note-66][^cite_note-67] The light originates from the accumulation of [unresolved](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angular_resolution) stars and other material located in the direction of the [galactic plane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galactic_plane). Brighter regions around the band appear as soft visual patches known as [star clouds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Star_cloud). The most conspicuous of these is the [Large Sagittarius Star Cloud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Large_Sagittarius_Star_Cloud), a portion of the central [bulge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galactic_bulge) of the galaxy.[^cite_note-68] Dark regions within the band, such as the [Great Rift](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Rift_\(astronomy\)) and the [Coalsack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalsack_Nebula), are areas where [interstellar dust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstellar_dust) blocks light from distant stars. Peoples of the southern hemisphere, including the [Inca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coricancha#Inca_astronomy) and [Australian Aboriginals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Aboriginal_astronomy#Emu_in_the_sky), identified these regions as [dark cloud constellations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dark_cloud_constellation).[^cite_note-69] The area of sky that the Milky Way obscures is called the [Zone of Avoidance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zone_of_Avoidance).[^cite_note-70]

The Milky Way has a relatively low [surface brightness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surface_brightness). Its visibility can be greatly reduced by background light, such as [light pollution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_pollution) or moonlight. The sky needs to be darker than about 20.2 [magnitude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnitude_\(astronomy\)) per square arcsecond for the Milky Way to be visible.[^cite_note-71] It should be visible if the [limiting magnitude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limiting_magnitude) is approximately +5.1 or better and shows a great deal of detail at +6.1.[^cite_note-steinicke_sakiel2007-72] This makes the Milky Way difficult to see from brightly lit urban or suburban areas, but very prominent when viewed from [rural areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rural_area) when the Moon is below the horizon.[^cite_note-bortle-73] Maps of artificial night sky brightness show that more than one-third of Earth's population cannot see the Milky Way from their homes due to light pollution.[^cite_note-74]

As viewed from Earth, the visible region of the Milky Way's [galactic plane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galactic_plane) occupies an area of the sky that includes 30 [constellations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constellation).[^cite_note-const-75] The [Galactic Center](373352.md) lies in the direction of [Sagittarius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sagittarius_\(constellation\)), where the Milky Way is brightest. From Sagittarius, the hazy band of white light appears to pass around to the [galactic anticenter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galactic_anticenter) in [Auriga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auriga_\(constellation\)). The band then continues the rest of the way around the sky, back to Sagittarius, dividing the sky into two roughly equal [hemispheres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celestial_sphere).[^cite_note-76]

The galactic plane is inclined by about 60° to the [ecliptic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecliptic) (the path of the Sun in the sky). It is tilted at an angle of 63° to the [celestial equator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celestial_equator).[^cite_note-77]

### Naked eye viewing

Naked eye viewing of the Milky Way as a band across the sky is based on several considerations:

- Because the galaxy is structured as a plane, a high density of stars is seen by looking into the plane, toward the Galactic Center. However, the other stars outside the band are also in the Milky Way.

- The galactic plane is 60 degrees tilted from the ecliptic of the Earth's orbit of the Sun. Thus, the band appears in the night sky at an angle to the horizon.

- As with all celestial object viewing, the Earth's orbit around the Sun affects the direction of view. The night sky is only facing the plane's center for a portion of the orbit; thus, the seasonality of viewing varies. The band is always there, but for a portion of the year, it is in the direction of the sun (daytime), so its light is washed out.

- As with all celestial object viewing, the Milky Way appears to rise over the horizon, rotate higher, and then fall over the horizon. This is caused by Earth's rotation, which brings the band into and out of our line of sight.

Viewing the Milky Way band of light and its Galactic Center requires the following conditions:[^cite_note-78]

- Eyes adapted to the dark sky (30 minutes)

- Low light pollution ([Bortle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bortle_scale) 4 or lower)

- Low or no moonlight (during a 5-day window of a new moon)

- Low level of haze and humidity, which may not be obvious

- Correct season for the hemisphere from which you are viewing (May–August is best in North America)

- Correct time of night; as with other celestial objects, it appears to rise and fall in the night sky.

- Correct viewing direction: in the Northern Hemisphere, it is in the south, between Sagittarius and Scorpius. In the southern hemisphere, it is near Carina and Centaurus.

## Astronomical history

See also: [Galaxy § Observation history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galaxy#Observation_history)

### Ancient, naked eye observations

Greek philosopher [Aristotle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotle) (384–322 BC) states in his *[Meteorologica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meteorology_\(Aristotle\))* that his predecessors [Anaxagoras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anaxagoras) (c. 500–428 BC) and [Democritus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democritus) (460–370 BC) had held the Milky Way to be the glow of stars not directly visible due to [Earth's shadow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth's_shadow), while other stars receive their light from the Sun, but have their glow obscured by solar rays.[^cite_note-79] Aristotle himself believed that the Milky Way was part of the Earth's upper atmosphere, along with the stars, and that it was a byproduct of stars burning that did not dissipate because of its outermost location in the atmosphere, composing its [great circle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_circle). He said that the milky appearance of the Milky Way Galaxy is due to the refraction of the Earth's atmosphere.[^cite_note-What_does_your_image_show-80][^cite_note-Montada-81][^cite_note-82] The [Neoplatonist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoplatonism) philosopher [Olympiodorus the Younger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympiodorus_the_Younger) (c. 495–570 AD) criticized this view, arguing that if the Milky Way were [sublunary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sublunary), it should appear different at different times and places on Earth, and that it should have [parallax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parallax), which it does not. In his view, the Milky Way is celestial. This idea would be influential later in the [Muslim world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_world).[^cite_note-heidarzadeh2008-83]

The [Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_people) astronomer [Al-Biruni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Biruni) (973–1048) proposed that the Milky Way is "a collection of countless fragments of the nature of [nebulous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nebula) stars".[^cite_note-mactutor_albirundi-84] The [Andalusian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Andalus) astronomer [Avempace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avempace) (died 1138) proposed that the Milky Way was made up of many stars but appeared to be a continuous image in the Earth's atmosphere, citing his observation of a [conjunction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conjunction_\(astronomy_and_astrology\)) of Jupiter and Mars in 1106 or 1107 as evidence.[^cite_note-Montada-81] The Persian astronomer [Nasir al-Din al-Tusi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nasir_al-Din_al-Tusi) (1201–1274) in his *Tadhkira* wrote: "The Milky Way, i.e. the Galaxy, is made up of a very large number of small, tightly clustered stars, which, on account of their concentration and smallness, seem to be cloudy patches. Because of this, it was likened to milk in color."[^cite_note-ragep1993-85] [Ibn Qayyim al-Jawziyya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_Qayyim_al-Jawziyya) (1292–1350) proposed that the Milky Way is "a myriad of tiny stars packed together in the sphere of the fixed stars".[^cite_note-Livingston-86]

### Telescopic observations



The shape of the Milky Way as deduced from star counts by [William Herschel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Herschel) in 1785. The [Solar System](26903.md) was assumed to be near the center.

Proof of the Milky Way as a system of many stars came in 1610, when [Galileo Galilei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galileo_Galilei) used an [optical telescope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Optical_telescope) to study the Milky Way and discovered that it was composed of a vast number of faint stars.[^cite_note-87] In a treatise in 1755, [Immanuel Kant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immanuel_Kant), misinterpreting [Thomas Wright](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Wright_\(astronomer\))'s suggestion that the Milky Way was shaped like a [balloon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balloon), speculated (correctly) that the Milky Way might be a rotating body of a huge number of stars, held together by [gravitational](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gravitation) forces akin to the Solar System but on much larger scales.[^cite_note-Sanders-2013-88]<sup>: 15 </sup> The resulting disk of stars would be seen as a band in the sky from our perspective inside the disk. Wright and Kant also conjectured that some of the [nebulae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nebula) visible in the night sky might be separate "galaxies" themselves, similar to our own.[^cite_note-Sanders-2013-88]<sup>: 16 </sup> Kant referred to both the Milky Way and the "extragalactic nebulae" as "island universes", a term still current up to the 1930s.[^cite_note-our_galaxy-89]

The first attempt to describe the shape of the Milky Way and the position of the Sun within it was carried out by [William Herschel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Herschel) in 1785 by carefully counting the number of stars in different regions of the visible sky. He produced a diagram of the Milky Way's shape, with the Solar System near its center.[^cite_note-Sanders-2013-88]<sup>: 16 </sup>

In 1845, [Lord Rosse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Parsons,_3rd_Earl_of_Rosse) constructed a new telescope and was able to distinguish individual point sources in what had been dim blobs known as nebulae and to determine the spiral structure of what is now called the [Whirlpool Galaxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whirlpool_Galaxy) (M51).[^cite_note-abbey-90][^cite_note-91]



Photograph of the "Great Andromeda Nebula" from 1899, later identified as the [Andromeda Galaxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andromeda_Galaxy)

In 1904, while studying the [proper motions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proper_motion) of stars, [Jacobus Kapteyn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacobus_Kapteyn) reported that these were not random, as was believed at the time; stars could be divided into two streams, moving in nearly opposite directions.[^cite_note-92] It was later realized that Kapteyn's data had been the first evidence of the rotation of the Milky Way,[^cite_note-93] which ultimately led to the finding of galactic rotation by [Bertil Lindblad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bertil_Lindblad) and [Jan Oort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jan_Oort).

In 1917, [Heber Doust Curtis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heber_Doust_Curtis) had observed the nova [S Andromedae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S_Andromedae) within the [Great Andromeda Nebula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Andromeda_Nebula) ([Messier object](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Messier_object) 31). Searching the photographic record, he found 11 more [novae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nova). Curtis noticed that these novae were, on average, 10 [magnitudes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnitude_\(astronomy\)) fainter than those that occurred within the Milky Way. As a result, he was able to come up with a distance estimate of 150,000 parsecs. He became a proponent of the "island universes" hypothesis, which held that the spiral nebulae were independent galaxies.[^cite_note-94][^cite_note-pasp100_6-95] In 1920 the [Great Debate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Debate_\(astronomy\)) took place between [Harlow Shapley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harlow_Shapley) and Heber Curtis, concerning the nature of the Milky Way, spiral nebulae, and the dimensions of the Universe. To support his claim that the Great Andromeda Nebula is an external galaxy, Curtis noted the appearance of dark lanes resembling the dust clouds in the Milky Way, as well as the significant [Doppler shift](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doppler_effect).[^cite_note-weaver-96]

The controversy was conclusively settled by [Edwin Hubble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edwin_Hubble) in the early 1920s using the Mount Wilson observatory [2.5 m (100 in) Hooker telescope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hooker_telescope). With the [light-gathering power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light-gathering_power) of this new telescope, he was able to produce [astronomical photographs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astrophotography) that resolved the outer parts of some spiral nebulae as collections of individual stars. He was also able to identify some [Cepheid variables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cepheid_variable) that he could use as a [benchmark](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/benchmark) to estimate the distance to the nebulae. He found that the Andromeda Nebula is 275,000 parsecs from the Sun, far too distant to be part of the Milky Way.[^cite_note-jrasc83_6-97][^cite_note-apj69_103-98]

### Satellite observations



Map of stars cataloged by the Gaia release in 2021, displayed as density mesh in the diagram

The [ESA](10363.md) spacecraft *[Gaia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaia_\(spacecraft\))* provides distance estimates by determining the [parallax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parallax) of a billion stars and is mapping the Milky Way.[^cite_note-99][^cite_note-100]

Data from *Gaia* has been described as "transformational". It has been estimated that *Gaia* has expanded the number of observed stars from about 2 million in the 1990s to 2 billion. It has expanded the measurable volume of space by a factor of 100 in radius and 1,000 in precision.[^cite_note-Skibba-101]

A study in 2020 concluded that *Gaia* detected a wobbling motion of the galaxy, which might be caused by "[torques](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torques) from a misalignment of the disc's rotation axis with respect to the principal axis of a non-spherical halo, or from [accreted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accretion_\(astrophysics\)) matter in the halo acquired during late infall, or from nearby, interacting satellite galaxies and their consequent tides".[^cite_note-poggio-31] In April 2024, initial studies and related maps, involving the [magnetic fields](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnetic_field) of the Milky Way were reported.[^cite_note-NYT-20240419-102]

## Astrography

### Sun's location and neighborhood

See also: [Location of Earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Location_of_Earth)



Map of stars cataloged by the *Gaia* release in 2021, overlay on top of artist's conception of the Milky Way overall shape

The Sun is near the inner rim of the [Orion Arm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orion_Arm), within the [Local Fluff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_Fluff) of the [Local Bubble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_Bubble), between the [Radcliffe wave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radcliffe_wave) and *Split* linear structures (formerly [Gould Belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gould_Belt)).[^cite_note-Alves_Zucker_Goodman_Speagle_2020-103] Based upon studies of stellar orbits around Sgr A\* by Gillessen *et al.* (2016), the Sun lies at an estimated distance of 27.14 ± 0.46 kly (8.32 ± 0.14 kpc)[^cite_note-Gillessen2016-40] from the Galactic Center. Boehle *et al.* (2016) found a smaller value of 25.64 ± 0.46 kly (7.86 ± 0.14 kpc), also using a star orbit analysis.[^cite_note-boehle2016-104] The Sun is currently 5–30 parsecs (16–98 ly) above, or north of, the central plane of the Galactic disk.[^cite_note-majaess2009-105] The distance between the local arm and the next arm out, the [Perseus Arm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perseus_Arm), is about 2,000 parsecs (6,500 ly).[^cite_note-fn9-106] The Sun, and thus the Solar System, is located in the Milky Way's [galactic habitable zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galactic_habitable_zone).[^cite_note-mullen-2001-107][^cite_note-sundin-2006-108]

There are about 208 stars brighter than [absolute magnitude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Absolute_magnitude) 8.5 within a sphere with a radius of 15 parsecs (49 ly) from the Sun, giving a density of one star per 69 cubic parsecs, or one star per 2,360 cubic [light-years](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light-year) (from [List of nearest bright stars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_nearest_bright_stars)). On the other hand, there are 64 known stars (of any magnitude, not counting 4 [brown dwarfs](44401.md)) within 5 parsecs (16 ly) of the Sun, giving a density of about one star per 8.2 cubic parsecs, or one per 284 cubic light-years (from [List of nearest stars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_nearest_stars)). This illustrates the fact that there are far more faint stars than bright stars: in the entire sky, there are about 500 stars brighter than [apparent magnitude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apparent_magnitude) 4 but 15.5 million stars brighter than apparent magnitude 14.[^cite_note-109]

The apex of the Sun's way, or the [solar apex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_apex), is the direction that the Sun travels through the [Local standard of rest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_standard_of_rest) in the Milky Way. The general direction of the Sun's Galactic motion is towards the star [Deneb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deneb) near the constellation of [Cygnus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cygnus_\(constellation\)), at an angle of roughly 90 sky degrees to the direction of the Galactic Center. The Sun's orbit about the Milky Way is expected to be roughly elliptical, with perturbations from the Galactic spiral arms and non-uniform mass distributions. In addition, the Sun passes through the Galactic plane approximately 2.7 times per orbit.[^cite_note-110] This is similar to how a [simple harmonic oscillator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simple_harmonic_oscillator) works with no drag force (damping) term. These oscillations were until recently thought to coincide with [mass lifeform extinction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_extinction) periods on Earth.[^cite_note-extinction-111] A reanalysis of the effects of the Sun's transit through the spiral structure based on CO data has failed to find a correlation.[^cite_note-overholt_etal-112]

It takes the Solar System about 240 million years to complete one orbit of the Milky Way (a [galactic year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galactic_year)),[^cite_note-sparke_gallagher-113] so the Sun is thought to have completed 18–20 orbits during its lifetime and 1/1250 of a revolution since the [origin of humans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Origin_of_humans). The [orbital speed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_speed) of the Solar System about the center of the Milky Way is approximately 220 km/s (490,000 mph) or 0.073% of the [speed of light](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speed_of_light). The Sun moves through the heliosphere at 84,000 km/h (52,000 mph). At this speed, it takes around 1,400 years for the Solar System to travel a distance of 1 light-year, or 8 days to travel 1 AU ([astronomical unit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astronomical_unit)).[^cite_note-garlick2002-114] The Solar System is headed in the direction of the zodiacal constellation [Scorpius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scorpius), which follows the ecliptic.[^cite_note-115]

### Galactic quadrants

Main article: [Galactic quadrant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galactic_quadrant)



A diagram of the Sun's location in the Milky Way; the angles represent longitudes in the [galactic coordinate system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galactic_coordinate_system)

A galactic quadrant, or quadrant of the Milky Way, refers to one of four circular sectors in the division of the Milky Way. In astronomical practice, the delineation of the galactic quadrants is based upon the [galactic coordinate system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galactic_coordinate_system), which places the [Sun](26751.md) as the [origin of the mapping system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polar_coordinate_system).[^cite_note-116]

Quadrants are described using [ordinals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ordinal_number_\(linguistics\)) – for example, "1st galactic quadrant",[^cite_note-toolsofradio-117] "second galactic quadrant",[^cite_note-NASAAD131K-118] or "third quadrant of the Milky Way".[^cite_note-Lampton1997-119] Viewing from the [north galactic pole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_galactic_pole) with 0° [(zero degrees)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Degree_\(angle\)) as the [ray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ray_\(geometry\)) that runs starting from the Sun and through the Galactic Center, the quadrants are:

| Galactic quadrant | Galactic longitude [(ℓ)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galactic_longitude) | Reference |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1st | 0° ≤ ℓ ≤ 90° | [^cite_note-vanWoerden-Strom-2006-120] |
| 2nd | 90° ≤ ℓ ≤ 180° | [^cite_note-NASAAD131K-118] |
| 3rd | 180° ≤ ℓ ≤ 270° | [^cite_note-Lampton1997-119] |
| 4th | 270° ≤ ℓ ≤ 360° (360° ≅ 0°) | [^cite_note-toolsofradio-117] |

with the galactic longitude [(ℓ)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galactic_longitude) increasing in the counter-clockwise direction ([positive rotation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angle#Positive_and_negative_angles)) as viewed from [north](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galactic_coordinate_system) of the [Galactic Center](373352.md) (a point several hundred thousand light-years distant from Earth in the direction of the constellation [Coma Berenices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coma_Berenices)); if viewed from south of the Galactic Center (a point similarly distant in the constellation [Sculptor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sculptor_\(constellation\))), [ℓ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galactic_longitude) would increase in the clockwise direction ([negative rotation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angle#Positive_and_negative_angles)).

## General characteristics

### Size



A size comparison of the six largest galaxies of the [Local Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_Group), including the Milky Way

The Milky Way is one of the two largest galaxies in the [Local Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_Group) (the other being the [Andromeda Galaxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andromeda_Galaxy)). However, the size of its [galactic disc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galactic_disc) and the extent to which it defines the isophotal diameter are not well understood.[^cite_note-Lopez2018-14] It is estimated that the significant bulk of stars in the galaxy lies within the 26 kiloparsecs (80,000 light-years) diameter, and that the number of stars beyond the outermost disc dramatically reduces to a very low number, with respect to an extrapolation of the exponential disk with the scale length of the inner disc.[^cite_note-arxiv0909.3857-121][^cite_note-Lopez2018-14]

There are several methods used in astronomy to define the size of a galaxy, and each can yield different results compared to the others. The most commonly employed method is the [D<sub>25</sub> standard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galaxy#Isophotal_diameter) – the [isophote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isophote) where the photometric brightness of a galaxy in the B-band (445 nm wavelength of light, in the blue part of the [visible spectrum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visible_spectrum)) reaches 25 mag/arcsec<sup>2</sup>.[^cite_note-Dimensions-122] An estimate from 1997 by Goodwin and others compared the distribution of [Cepheid variable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cepheid_variable) stars in 17 other spiral galaxies to the ones in the Milky Way, and modelling the relationship to their surface brightnesses. This gave an [isophotal diameter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isophotal_diameter) for the Milky Way at 26.8 ± 1.1 kiloparsecs (87,400 ± 3,600 light-years), by assuming that an exponential disc well represents the galactic disc and adopting a central surface brightness of the galaxy (μ<sub>0</sub>) of 22.1±0.3 *B*-mag/arcsec<sup>−2</sup> and a disk scale length (*h*) of 5.0 ± 0.5 kpc (16,300 ± 1,600 ly).[^cite_note-Average-123][^cite_note-Goodwin-13][^cite_note-Castro-124]

This is significantly smaller than the Andromeda Galaxy's isophotal diameter, and slightly below the mean isophotal sizes of the galaxies, being at 28.3 kpc (92,000 ly).[^cite_note-Goodwin-13] The paper concludes that the Milky Way and Andromeda Galaxy were not overly large spiral galaxies, nor were they among the [largest known](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_galaxies) (if the former not being the largest) as previously widely believed, but rather average ordinary spiral galaxies.[^cite_note-Goodwin_Hubble-125] To compare the relative physical scale of the Milky Way, if the [Solar System](26903.md) out to [Neptune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neptune) were the size of a [US quarter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quarter_\(United_States_coin\)) (24.3 mm or 0.955 in), the Milky Way would be approximately at least the greatest north–south line of the [contiguous United States](83759.md).[^cite_note-126] An even older study from 1978 gave a lower diameter for Milky Way of about 23 kpc (75,000 ly).[^cite_note-Goodwin-13]

A 2015 paper reported that there is a ring-like filament of stars called Triangulum–Andromeda Ring (TriAnd Ring) rippling above and below the relatively flat [galactic plane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galactic_plane), which alongside [Monoceros Ring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monoceros_Ring) were both suggested to be primarily the result of disk oscillations and wrapping around the Milky Way, at a diameter of at least 50 kpc (160,000 ly),[^cite_note-127] which may be part of the Milky Way's outer disk itself, hence making the stellar disk larger by increasing to this size.[^cite_note-rpi2015-128] A more recent 2018 paper later somewhat ruled out this hypothesis, and supported a conclusion that the Monoceros Ring, [A13](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A13_\(stellar_overdensity\)?action=edit\&redlink=1) and TriAnd Ring were stellar overdensities rather kicked out from the main stellar disk, with the velocity dispersion of the RR Lyrae stars found to be higher and consistent with halo membership.[^cite_note-allyson-129]

Another 2018 study revealed the very probable presence of disk stars at 26–31.5 kpc (84,800–103,000 ly) from the Galactic Center or perhaps even farther, significantly beyond approximately 13–20 kpc (40,000–70,000 ly), in which it was once believed to be the abrupt drop-off of the stellar density of the disk, meaning that few or no stars were expected to be above this limit, save for stars that belong to the old population of the galactic halo.[^cite_note-Lopez2018-14][^cite_note-nbcnews1-130][^cite_note-131]

A 2020 study predicted the edge of the Milky Way's [dark matter halo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dark_matter_halo) being around 292 ± 61 [kpc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parsec#Parsecs_and_kiloparsecs) (952,000 ± 199,000 [ly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light-year)), which translates to a diameter of 584 ± 122 [kpc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parsec#Parsecs_and_kiloparsecs) (1.905 ± 0.3979 [Mly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light-year#Definitions)).[^cite_note-croswell2020-32][^cite_note-dearson2020-33] The Milky Way's stellar disk is also estimated to be approximately up to 1.35 kpc (4,000 ly) thick.[^cite_note-ask-astro-132][^cite_note-Rix_Bovy-133]

### Mass



A schematic profile of the Milky Way.\
Abbreviations: GNP/GSP: Galactic North and South Poles

The Milky Way is approximately 0.88 trillion times the mass of the Sun in total (8.8×10<sup>11</sup> solar masses), using a cutoff of 200kpc to define the galaxy.[^cite_note-134] Estimates of the mass of the Milky Way vary, depending upon the method and data used. The low end of the estimate range is 5.8×10<sup>11</sup> [solar masses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_mass) (M<sub>☉</sub>), somewhat less than that of the [Andromeda Galaxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andromeda_Galaxy).[^cite_note-Karachentsevetal2006-135][^cite_note-mass-136][^cite_note-galmass-137]

Measurements using the [Very Long Baseline Array](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Very_Long_Baseline_Array) in 2009 found velocities as high as 254 km/s (570,000 mph) for stars at the outer edge of the Milky Way.[^cite_note-nrao20090105-138] Because the orbital velocity depends on the total mass inside the orbital radius, this suggests that the Milky Way is more massive, roughly equaling the mass of the Andromeda Galaxy at 7×10<sup>11</sup> M<sub>☉</sub> within 160,000 ly (49 kpc) of its center.[^cite_note-reid2009-139] In 2010, a measurement of the radial velocity of halo stars found that the mass enclosed within 80 kilo[parsecs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parsec) is 7×10<sup>11</sup> M<sub>☉</sub>.[^cite_note-gnedin2010-140] In a 2014 study, the mass of the entire Milky Way is estimated to be 8.5×10<sup>11</sup> M<sub>☉</sub>,[^cite_note-Jorge_Peñarrubia2014-141] but this is only half the mass of the Andromeda Galaxy.[^cite_note-Jorge_Peñarrubia2014-141] A recent 2019 mass estimate for the Milky Way is 1.29×10<sup>12</sup> M<sub>☉</sub>.[^cite_note-142]

Much of the mass of the Milky Way seems to be [dark matter](8651.md), an unknown and invisible form of matter that interacts gravitationally with ordinary matter. A [dark matter halo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dark_matter_halo) is conjectured to spread out relatively uniformly to a distance beyond one hundred kiloparsecs (kpc) from the Galactic Center. Mathematical models of the Milky Way suggest that the mass of dark matter is 1–1.5×10<sup>12</sup> M<sub>☉</sub>.[^cite_note-McMillan2011-143][^cite_note-McMillan2016-144][^cite_note-145] 2013 and 2014 studies indicate a range in mass, as large as 4.5×10<sup>12</sup> M<sub>☉</sub>[^cite_note-146] and as small as 8×10<sup>11</sup> M<sub>☉</sub>.[^cite_note-Kafle2014-24] By comparison, the total mass of all the stars in the Milky Way is estimated to be between 4.6×10<sup>10</sup> M<sub>☉</sub>[^cite_note-Licquia2013-147] and 6.43×10<sup>10</sup> M<sub>☉</sub>.[^cite_note-McMillan2011-143]

In addition to the stars, there is also interstellar gas, comprising 90% [hydrogen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrogen) and 10% [helium](13256.md) by mass,[^cite_note-ism1-148] with two-thirds of the hydrogen found in the [atomic form](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neutral_hydrogen) and the remaining one-third as [molecular hydrogen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molecular_hydrogen).[^cite_note-ism2-149] The mass of the Milky Way's interstellar gas is equal to between 10%[^cite_note-ism2-149] and 15%[^cite_note-ism1-148] of the total mass of its stars. [Interstellar dust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstellar_dust) accounts for an additional 1% of the total mass of the gas.[^cite_note-ism1-148]

In March 2019, astronomers reported that the [virial mass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virial_mass) of the Milky Way Galaxy is 1.54×10<sup>12</sup> solar masses within a [radius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radius) of about 39.5 kpc (130,000 ly), over twice as much as was determined in earlier studies, suggesting that about 90% of the mass of the galaxy is dark matter.[^cite_note-SA-20190308-8][^cite_note-ARX-20190312-9]

In September 2023, astronomers reported that the virial mass of the Milky Way Galaxy is only 2.06×10<sup>11</sup> solar masses, only a tenth of the mass of previous studies. The mass was determined from data of the [*Gaia* spacecraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaia_\(spacecraft\)).[^cite_note-aA-20230927-150]

### Rotation curve



[Galaxy rotation curve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galaxy_rotation_curve) for the Milky Way. The vertical axis is the rotation speed about the galactic center. The horizontal axis is the distance from the galactic center. The Sun is marked in yellow. Data points mark the observed rotation speed curve. The predicted curve based on the stellar mass and gas of the Milky Way is in black. The difference is due to [dark matter](8651.md) or possibly a modification of gravity like [MOND](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modified_Newtonian_Dynamics). The data shown can be found here.

[^cite_note-i331-151]

[^cite_note-h438-152]

[^cite_note-i877-153]

[^cite_note-j479-154]

[^cite_note-f082-155]

[^cite_note-p712-156]

[^cite_note-y832-157]

[^cite_note-c301-158]

[^cite_note-s826-159]

[^cite_note-ARX-20190312-9]

The stars and gas in the Milky Way rotate about its center [differentially](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Differential_rotation), meaning that the rotation period varies with location. As is typical for spiral galaxies, the orbital speed of most stars in the Milky Way does not depend strongly on their distance from the center. Away from the central bulge or outer rim, the typical stellar orbital speed is between 200 and 220 km/s.[^cite_note-160] Hence the [orbital period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_period) of the typical star is approximately proportional to the length of the path traveled. This is unlike the situation in the Solar System, where two-body gravitational dynamics dominate, and different orbits have significantly different velocities. The rotation curve (shown in the figure) describes this rotation.

If the Milky Way contained only the mass observed in stars, gas, and other baryonic (ordinary) matter, the rotational speed would decrease with distance from the center. However, the observed curve is relatively flat, suggesting additional mass that cannot be directly detected with electromagnetic radiation. This inconsistency is attributed to dark matter.[^cite_note-Koupelis_Kuhn2007-161] The rotation curve of the Milky Way agrees with the [universal rotation curve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_rotation_curve) of spiral galaxies, the best evidence for the existence of dark matter in galaxies. Alternatively, a minority of astronomers propose that a [modification of the law of gravity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MOND) may explain the observed rotation curve.[^cite_note-Schneider_p413-162]

### Peculiar velocity

Although [special relativity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_relativity) states that there is no "preferred" [inertial frame of reference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inertial_frame_of_reference) in space with which to compare the Milky Way, the Milky Way does have a velocity with respect to cosmological [frames of reference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frames_of_reference).[^cite_note-163]

One such frame of reference is the [Hubble flow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hubble_flow), the apparent motions of galaxy clusters due to the [expansion of space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expansion_of_space). Individual galaxies, including the Milky Way, have [peculiar velocities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peculiar_velocities) relative to the average flow. Thus, to compare the Milky Way to the Hubble flow, one must consider a volume large enough so that the expansion of the Universe dominates over local, random motions. A large enough volume means that the mean motion of galaxies within this volume is equal to the Hubble flow. Astronomers believe the Milky Way is moving at approximately 630 km/s (1,400,000 mph) with respect to this local co-moving frame of reference.[^cite_note-164][^cite_note-165]

The Milky Way is moving in the general direction of the [Great Attractor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Attractor) and other [galaxy clusters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galaxy_cluster), including the [Shapley Supercluster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shapley_Supercluster), behind it.[^cite_note-166] The Local Group, a cluster of gravitationally bound galaxies containing, among others, the Milky Way and the Andromeda Galaxy, is part of a [supercluster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supercluster) called the [Local Supercluster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_Supercluster), centered near the [Virgo Cluster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virgo_Cluster): although they are moving away from each other at 967 km/s (2,160,000 mph) as part of the Hubble flow, this velocity is less than would be expected given the 16.8 million pc distance due to the gravitational attraction between the Local Group and the Virgo Cluster.[^cite_note-167]

Another reference frame is provided by the [cosmic microwave background](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cosmic_microwave_background) (CMB), in which the CMB temperature is least distorted by Doppler shift (zero dipole moment). The Milky Way is moving at 552 ± 6 km/s (1,235,000 ± 13,000 mph)[^cite_note-COBE1993-23] with respect to this frame, toward 10.5 right ascension, −24° declination ([J2000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J2000) epoch, near the center of [Hydra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydra_\(constellation\))). This motion is observed by satellites such as the [Cosmic Background Explorer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cosmic_Background_Explorer) (COBE) and the [Wilkinson Microwave Anisotropy Probe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wilkinson_Microwave_Anisotropy_Probe) (WMAP) as a dipole contribution to the CMB, as photons in equilibrium in the CMB frame get [blue-shifted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doppler_effect) in the direction of the motion and [red-shifted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Redshift) in the opposite direction.[^cite_note-COBE1993-23]

## Contents



The [Galactic Center](373352.md), as seen by one of the [2MASS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2MASS) infrared telescopes, is located in the bright upper left portion of the image.

The Milky Way contains between 100 and 400 billion stars[^cite_note-frommert_kronberg2005-11][^cite_note-wethington2010-12] and at least that many planets.[^cite_note-villard20120111-168] An exact figure would depend on counting the number of very-low-mass stars, which are difficult to detect, especially at distances of more than 300 ly (90 pc) from the Sun. As a comparison, the neighboring Andromeda Galaxy contains an estimated one trillion (10<sup>12</sup>) stars.[^cite_note-young2006-169] The Milky Way may contain ten billion [white dwarfs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_dwarf), a billion [neutron stars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neutron_star), and a hundred million stellar [black holes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_hole).[^cite_note-number-172][^cite_note-173][^cite_note-174] Filling the space between the stars is a disk of gas and dust called the [interstellar medium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstellar_medium). This disk has at least a comparable extent in radius to the stars,[^cite_note-science312_1773-175] whereas the thickness of the gas layer ranges from hundreds of light-years for the colder gas to thousands of light-years for the warmer gas.[^cite_note-araa28_215-176][^cite_note-apj702_1472-177]

The disk of stars in the Milky Way does not have a sharp edge beyond which there are no stars. Rather, the concentration of stars decreases with distance from the center of the Milky Way. Beyond a radius of roughly 40,000 light-years (13 kpc) from the center, the number of stars per cubic parsec drops much faster with radius.[^cite_note-arxiv0909.3857-121] Surrounding the galactic disk is a spherical [galactic halo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galactic_halo) of stars and [globular clusters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globular_cluster) that extends farther outward, but is limited in size by the orbits of two Milky Way satellites, the Large and Small [Magellanic Clouds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magellanic_Clouds), whose [closest approach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apsis) to the Galactic Center is about 180,000 ly (55 kpc).[^cite_note-mnras371_1_108-178] At this distance or beyond, the orbits of most halo objects would be disrupted by the Magellanic Clouds. Hence, such objects would probably be ejected from the vicinity of the Milky Way. The integrated [absolute visual magnitude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Absolute_visual_magnitude) of the Milky Way is estimated to be around −20.9.[^cite_note-coffey-179][^cite_note-Karachentsev2004-180][^cite_note-milky_way_mag-181]

Both [gravitational microlensing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gravitational_microlensing) and planetary transit observations indicate that there may be at least as many planets bound to stars as there are stars in the Milky Way,[^cite_note-Nature-20120111-38][^cite_note-borenstein2011-182] and microlensing measurements indicate that there are more [rogue planets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rogue_planet) not bound to host stars than there are stars.[^cite_note-183][^cite_note-nasa20110526-184] The Milky Way contains an average of at least one planet per star, resulting in 100–400 billion planets, according to a January 2013 study of the five-planet star system [Kepler-32](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kepler-32) by the [Kepler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kepler_\(spacecraft\)) space observatory.[^cite_note-Space-20130102-39] A different January 2013 analysis of Kepler data estimated that at least 17 billion [Earth-sized](48510.md) [exoplanets](9763.md) reside in the Milky Way.[^cite_note-Space-Billions-185]

In November 2013, astronomers reported, based on [Kepler space telescope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kepler_\(spacecraft\)) data, that there could be as many as 40 billion Earth-sized [planets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extrasolar_planet) orbiting in the [habitable zones](1072751.md) of [Sun-like stars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_analog) and [red dwarfs](56099.md) within the Milky Way.[^cite_note-NYT-20131104-186][^cite_note-PNAS-20131031-187][^cite_note-nov2013_planet_estimate-188] 11 billion of these estimated planets may be orbiting Sun-like stars.[^cite_note-LATimes-20131104-189] The nearest exoplanet may be 4.2 light-years away, orbiting the [red dwarf](56099.md) [Proxima Centauri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proxima_Centauri), according to a 2016 study.[^cite_note-Planet-Alpha-Centauri-190] Such Earth-sized planets may be more numerous than gas giants,[^cite_note-Nature-20120111-38] though harder to detect at great distances given their small size. Besides exoplanets, "[exocomets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exocomet)", [comets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comet) beyond the Solar System, have also been detected and may be common in the Milky Way.[^cite_note-Space-Exocomets-191] More recently, in November 2020, over 300 million habitable exoplanets are estimated to exist in the Milky Way Galaxy.[^cite_note-NYT-20201105-192]

When compared to other more distant galaxies in the universe, the Milky Way galaxy has a below average amount of [neutrino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neutrino) luminosity making our galaxy a "neutrino desert".[^cite_note-193]

## Structure



Overview of different elements of the overall structure of the Milky Way

The Milky Way consists of a bar-shaped core region surrounded by a warped disk of [gas, dust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstellar_medium) and stars.[^cite_note-194][^cite_note-195] The mass distribution within the Milky Way closely resembles the type Sbc in the [Hubble classification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hubble_sequence), which represents spiral galaxies with relatively loosely wound arms.[^cite_note-ssr100_1_129-6] Astronomers first began to conjecture that the Milky Way is a [barred spiral galaxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barred_spiral_galaxy), rather than an ordinary [spiral galaxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spiral_galaxy), in the 1960s.[^cite_note-196][^cite_note-197][^cite_note-198] These conjectures were confirmed by the [Spitzer Space Telescope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spitzer_Space_Telescope) observations in 2005 that showed the Milky Way's central bar to be larger than previously thought.[^cite_note-fn3-199]

### Galactic Center

Main articles: [Galactic Center](373352.md) and [Sagittarius A\*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sagittarius_A*)

A dark spot surrounded by a doughnut-shaped orange-yellow ring

[Supermassive black hole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supermassive_black_hole) [Sagittarius A\*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sagittarius_A*) imaged by the [Event Horizon Telescope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Event_Horizon_Telescope) in radio waves. The central dark spot is the black hole's shadow, which is larger than the [event horizon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Event_horizon).



Bright [X-ray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/X-ray) flares from Sagittarius A\* (inset) in the center of the Milky Way, as detected by the [Chandra X-ray Observatory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chandra_X-ray_Observatory)

[^cite_note-NASA-20150105-200]

The Sun is 25,000–28,000 ly (7.7–8.6 kpc) from the Galactic Center. This value is estimated using geometric-based methods or by measuring selected astronomical objects that serve as [standard candles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_candles), with different techniques yielding various values within this approximate range.[^cite_note-apj692_2_1075-201][^cite_note-boehle2016-104][^cite_note-Gillessen2016-40][^cite_note-ReideaSgrb2009-202][^cite_note-vanhollebeke09-203][^cite_note-majaess10-204] In the inner few kiloparsecs (around 10,000 light-years radius) is a dense concentration of mostly old stars in a roughly spheroidal shape called [the bulge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulge_\(astronomy\)).[^cite_note-Galactic_bulge-205] It has been proposed that the Milky Way lacks a [bulge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galactic_bulge#Classical_bulges) due to a [collision and merger between previous galaxies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galactic_merger), and that instead it only has a [*pseudobulge*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galactic_bulge#Disk-like_Bulges) formed by its central bar.[^cite_note-apjl_1_l72-206] However, confusion in the literature between the (peanut shell)-shaped structure created by instabilities in the bar, versus a possible bulge with an expected half-light radius of 0.5 kpc, abounds.[^cite_note-207]

The Galactic Center is marked by an intense [radio source](82961.md) named [Sagittarius A\*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sagittarius_A*) (pronounced *Sagittarius A-star*). The motion of material around the center indicates that Sagittarius A\* harbors a massive, compact object.[^cite_note-Jones2004-208] This concentration of mass is best explained as a [supermassive black hole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supermassive_black_hole)[^cite_note-chandra2003-209][^cite_note-apj692_2_1075-201][^cite_note-apj689_2_1044-210] (SMBH) with an estimated mass of 4.1–4.5 million times the [mass of the Sun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_mass).[^cite_note-apj689_2_1044-210] The rate of accretion of the SMBH is consistent with an [inactive galactic nucleus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Active_galactic_nucleus), being estimated at 1×10<sup>−5</sup> M<sub>☉</sub> per year.[^cite_note-wang13-211] Observations indicate that there are SMBHs located near the center of most normal galaxies.[^cite_note-blandford1999-212][^cite_note-frolov_zelnikov2011-213]

The nature of the Milky Way's bar is actively debated, with estimates for its half-length and orientation spanning from 1 to 5 kpc (3,000–16,000 ly) and 10–50 degrees relative to the line of sight from Earth to the Galactic Center.[^cite_note-vanhollebeke09-203][^cite_note-majaess10-204][^cite_note-Cabrera-Lavers08-214] Certain authors advocate that the Milky Way features two distinct bars, one nestled within the other.[^cite_note-nishiyama06-215] However, [RR Lyrae-type](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RR_Lyrae_variable) stars do not trace a prominent Galactic bar.[^cite_note-majaess10-204][^cite_note-alcock98-216][^cite_note-kunder08-217] The bar may be surrounded by a ring called the "5 kpc ring" that contains a large fraction of the molecular hydrogen present in the Milky Way, as well as most of the Milky Way's [star formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Star_formation) activity. Viewed from the [Andromeda Galaxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andromeda_Galaxy), it would be the brightest feature of the Milky Way.[^cite_note-fn14-218] X-ray emission from the core is aligned with the massive stars surrounding the central bar[^cite_note-wang13-211] and the [Galactic ridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galactic_ridge).[^cite_note-nature318_267-219]

In June 2023, astronomers led by [Naoko Kurahashi Neilson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naoko_Kurahashi_Neilson) reported using a new cascade neutrino technique[^cite_note-220] to detect, for the first time, the release of [neutrinos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neutrino) from the [galactic plane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galactic_plane) of the Milky Way [galaxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galaxy), creating the first neutrino view of the Milky Way.[^cite_note-NYT-20230629-221][^cite_note-SCI-20230629-222]

#### Gamma rays and X-rays



All-sky x-ray image

Since 1970, various gamma-ray detection missions have discovered 511-[keV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KeV) [gamma rays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamma_rays) coming from the general direction of the Galactic Center. These gamma rays are produced by [positrons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Positrons) (antielectrons) annihilating with [electrons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electron). In 2008, it was found that the distribution of gamma-ray sources resembles that of low-mass [X-ray binaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/X-ray_binaries), suggesting that these X-ray binaries are sending positrons (and electrons) into interstellar space, where they slow and annihilate.[^cite_note-223][^cite_note-NASA-2008-224][^cite_note-225] The observations were made by both [NASA](18426568.md) and [ESA](10363.md)'s satellites. In 1970, gamma-ray detectors found that the emitting region was about 10,000 light-years across, with a luminosity of about 10,000 times that of the Sun.[^cite_note-NASA-2008-224]



Illustration of the two gigantic [X-ray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/X-ray)/[gamma-ray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamma-ray) bubbles (blue-violet) of the Milky Way (center)

In 2010, two gigantic spherical bubbles of high-energy gamma-emission were detected to the north and the south of the Milky Way core, using data from the [Fermi Gamma-ray Space Telescope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fermi_Gamma-ray_Space_Telescope). The diameter of each of the bubbles is about 25,000 light-years (7.7 kpc) (or about 1/4 of the galaxy's estimated diameter); they stretch up to [Grus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grus_\(constellation\)) and to [Virgo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virgo_\(constellation\)) on the night-sky of the Southern Hemisphere.[^cite_note-overbye2010-226][^cite_note-sz2010-227] Subsequently, observations with the [Parkes Telescope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parkes_Telescope) at radio frequencies identified polarized emission that is associated with the Fermi bubbles. These observations are best interpreted as a magnetized outflow driven by star formation in the central 640 ly (200 pc) of the Milky Way.[^cite_note-228]

Later, on January 5, 2015, [NASA](18426568.md) reported observing an [X-ray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/X-ray) flare 400 times brighter than usual, a record-breaker, from Sagittarius A\*. The unusual event may have been caused by the breaking apart of an [asteroid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asteroid) falling into the black hole or by the entanglement of [magnetic field lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnetic_field_lines) within gas flowing into Sagittarius A\*.[^cite_note-NASA-20150105-200]

### Spiral arms

Further information: [Spiral galaxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spiral_galaxy)



Observed (normal lines) and extrapolated (dotted lines) structure of the spiral arms of the Milky Way, viewed from north of the galaxy – the galaxy rotates clockwise in this view. The gray lines radiating from the Sun's position (upper center) list the three-letter abbreviations of the corresponding constellations.

&#x20;Outside the gravitational influence of the Galactic bar, the structure of the interstellar medium and stars in the disk of the Milky Way is organized into four spiral arms.[^cite_note-churchwell09-229] Spiral arms typically contain a higher density of interstellar gas and dust than the Galactic average as well as a greater concentration of star formation, as traced by [H II regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H_II_region)[^cite_note-TaylorCordes-230][^cite_note-russeil03-231] and [molecular clouds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molecular_cloud).[^cite_note-232]

The Milky Way's spiral structure is uncertain, and there is currently no consensus on the nature of its arms.[^cite_note-two_arms-233] Perfect logarithmic spiral patterns only crudely describe features near the Sun,[^cite_note-russeil03-231][^cite_note-majaess09b-234] because galaxies commonly have arms that branch, merge, twist unexpectedly, and feature a degree of irregularity.[^cite_note-majaess10-204][^cite_note-majaess09b-234][^cite_note-lepine10-235] The possible scenario of the Sun within a spur / Local arm[^cite_note-russeil03-231] emphasizes that point and indicates that such features are probably not unique, and exist elsewhere in the Milky Way.[^cite_note-majaess09b-234] Estimates of the pitch angle of the arms range from about 7° to 25°.[^cite_note-science312_1773-175][^cite_note-Drimmel2000-236] There are thought to be four spiral arms that all start near the galaxy's center.[^cite_note-237] These are named as follows, with the positions of the arms shown in the image:

| Color | Arm(s) |
| --- | --- |
| turquoise | [Near 3 kpc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Near_3_kpc_Arm) and [Perseus Arm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perseus_Arm) |
| blue | [Norma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norma_Arm) and [Outer arm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outer_arm) (Along with extension discovered in 2004[^cite_note-mcclure-griffiths-238]) |
| green | [Far 3 kpc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_3_kpc_Arm) and [Scutum–Centaurus Arm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scutum%E2%80%93Centaurus_Arm) |
| red | [Carina–Sagittarius Arm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carina%E2%80%93Sagittarius_Arm) |
| *There are at least two smaller arms or spurs, including:* |  |
| orange | [Orion–Cygnus Arm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orion%E2%80%93Cygnus_Arm) (which contains the Sun and Solar System) |

Two spiral arms, the Scutum–Centaurus arm and the Carina–Sagittarius arm, have tangent points inside the Sun's orbit about the center of the Milky Way. If these arms contain an overdensity of stars compared to the average density of stars in the Galactic disk, it would be detectable by counting the stars near the tangent point. Two surveys of near-infrared light, which is sensitive primarily to red giants and not affected by dust extinction, detected the predicted overabundance in the Scutum–Centaurus arm but not in the Carina–Sagittarius arm: the Scutum–Centaurus Arm contains approximately 30% more [red giants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_giant) than would be expected in the absence of a spiral arm.[^cite_note-Drimmel2000-236][^cite_note-Benjamin2005-239]

This observation suggests that the Milky Way possesses only two major stellar arms: the Perseus arm and the Scutum–Centaurus arm. The rest of the arms contain excess gas but not excess old stars.[^cite_note-two_arms-233] In December 2013, astronomers found that the distribution of young stars and star-forming regions matches the four-arm spiral description of the Milky Way.[^cite_note-240][^cite_note-241][^cite_note-urquart2013-242] Thus, the Milky Way appears to have two spiral arms as traced by old stars and four spiral arms as traced by gas and young stars. The explanation for this apparent discrepancy is unclear.[^cite_note-urquart2013-242]

The *[Near 3 kpc Arm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Near_3_kpc_Arm)* (also called the *Expanding 3 kpc Arm* or simply the *3 kpc Arm*) was discovered in the 1950s by astronomer van Woerden and collaborators through [21 centimeter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrogen_line) radio measurements of H<sup>I</sup> ([atomic hydrogen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_hydrogen)).[^cite_note-243][^cite_note-Dame-244] It was found to be expanding away from the central bulge at more than 50 [km/s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kilometer_per_second). It is located in the fourth galactic quadrant at a distance of about 5.2 [kpc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parsec) from the [Sun](26751.md) and 3.3 kpc from the [Galactic Center](373352.md). The Far 3 kpc Arm was discovered in 2008 by astronomer Tom Dame ([Center for Astrophysics | Harvard & Smithsonian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvard%E2%80%93Smithsonian_Center_for_Astrophysics)). It is located in the first galactic quadrant at a distance of 3 kpc (about 10,000 [ly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_year)) from the Galactic Center.[^cite_note-Dame-244][^cite_note-245]

A simulation published in 2011 suggested that the Milky Way may have obtained its spiral arm structure as a result of repeated collisions with the [Sagittarius Dwarf Elliptical Galaxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sagittarius_Dwarf_Elliptical_Galaxy).[^cite_note-246]

It has been suggested that the Milky Way contains two different spiral patterns: an inner one, formed by the Sagittarius arm, that rotates fast, and an outer one, formed by the Carina and Perseus arms, whose rotation velocity is slower and whose arms are tightly wound. In this scenario, suggested by numerical simulations of the dynamics of the different spiral arms, the outer pattern would form an outer [pseudoring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galaxy_morphological_classification#De_Vaucouleurs_system),[^cite_note-pseudoring2-247] and the two patterns would be connected by the Cygnus arm.[^cite_note-pseudoring-248]

Outside of the major spiral arms is the [Monoceros Ring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monoceros_Ring) (or Outer Ring), a ring of gas and stars torn from other galaxies billions of years ago. However, several members of the scientific community recently restated their position, affirming the Monoceros structure is nothing more than an over-density produced by the flared and warped [thick disk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thick_disk) of the Milky Way.[^cite_note-249] The structure of the Milky Way's disk is warped along an ["S" curve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sigmoid_function).[^cite_note-250]

### Halo

Main article: [Galactic halo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galactic_halo)

The Galactic disk is surrounded by a [spheroidal halo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galactic_spheroid) of old stars and [globular clusters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globular_cluster) and contains most of the Milky Way's [dark matter](8651.md). It is moderately [triaxial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triaxial_ellipsoid) in shape, with the longest axis tilted about 25° above the disk and 24° ahead of the Sun. Its density seems to obey a [power law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_law) with two breaks at around 15 and 30 kiloparsecs (49 and 98 kly).[^cite_note-251] However, a few globular clusters have been found farther, such as PAL 4 and AM 1 at more than 200,000 light-years from the Galactic Center. About 40% of the Milky Way's clusters are on [retrograde orbits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Retrograde_orbit), which means they move in the opposite direction from the Milky Way rotation.[^cite_note-aaa313_119-252] The globular clusters can follow [rosette orbits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosette_orbit) about the Milky Way, in contrast to the [elliptical orbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elliptical_orbit) of a planet around a star.[^cite_note-apj522-253]

Although the disk contains dust that obscures the view at some wavelengths, the halo component does not. Active [star formation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Star_formation) takes place in the disk (especially in the spiral arms, which represent areas of high density), but does not take place in the halo, as there is little cool gas to collapse into stars.[^cite_note-sparke_gallagher-113] [Open clusters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open_cluster) are also located primarily on the disk.[^cite_note-aj108-254]

Discoveries in the early 21st century have added new dimensions to our understanding of the Milky Way's structure. With the discovery that the disk of the Andromeda Galaxy (M31) extends much farther than previously thought,[^cite_note-fn5-255] the possibility of the disk of the Milky Way extending farther is apparent, and this is supported by evidence from the discovery of the Outer Arm extension of the [Cygnus Arm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cygnus_Arm)[^cite_note-mcclure-griffiths-238][^cite_note-fn6-256] and of a similar extension of the [Scutum–Centaurus Arm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scutum%E2%80%93Centaurus_Arm).[^cite_note-257] With the discovery of the [Sagittarius Dwarf Elliptical Galaxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sagittarius_Dwarf_Elliptical_Galaxy) came the discovery of a ribbon of galactic debris as the polar orbit of the dwarf and its interaction with the Milky Way tears it apart. Upon the 2004 discovery of a ring of galactic debris in an in-plane orbit around the Milky Way, it was initially believed that the debris was the remnant of a system dubbed the [Canis Major Dwarf Galaxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canis_Major_Dwarf_Galaxy).[^cite_note-258] Other scholars believed it to be due to the Galactic warp,[^cite_note-259] a view which has been supported by more recent evidence as of 2021.[^cite_note-260]

The [Sloan Digital Sky Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sloan_Digital_Sky_Survey) of the northern sky shows a huge, diffuse structure (spread across an area about 5,000 times the size of a full moon) within the Milky Way that does not seem to fit current models. The collection of stars rises nearly perpendicular to the plane of the Milky Way's spiral arms. The proposed likely interpretation is that a [dwarf galaxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dwarf_galaxy) is merging with the Milky Way. This galaxy is tentatively named the [Virgo Stellar Stream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virgo_Stellar_Stream) and is found in the direction of Virgo about 30,000 light-years (9 kpc) away.[^cite_note-apj673_2_864-261]

#### Gaseous halo

In addition to the stellar halo, the [Chandra X-ray Observatory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chandra_X-ray_Observatory), [XMM-Newton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/XMM-Newton), and [*Suzaku*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suzaku_\(satellite\)) have provided evidence that there is also a gaseous halo containing a large amount of hot gas. This halo extends for hundreds of thousands of light-years, much farther than the stellar halo and close to the distance of the Large and Small [Magellanic Clouds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magellanic_Clouds). The mass of this hot halo is nearly equivalent to the mass of the Milky Way itself.[^cite_note-Harrington_feature-262][^cite_note-263][^cite_note-264] The temperature of this halo gas is between 1 and 2.5 million K (1.8 and 4.5 million °F).[^cite_note-265]

Observations of distant galaxies indicate that the Universe had about one-sixth as much [baryonic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baryon) (ordinary) matter as dark matter when it was just a few billion years old. However, only about half of those baryons are accounted for in the modern Universe based on observations of nearby galaxies like the Milky Way.[^cite_note-Harrington_feature-262] If the finding that the mass of the halo is comparable to the mass of the Milky Way is confirmed, it could be the identity of the missing baryons around the Milky Way.[^cite_note-Harrington_feature-262]

## Formation

Main article: [Galaxy formation and evolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galaxy_formation_and_evolution)

### History



A [galaxy color–magnitude diagram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galaxy_color%E2%80%93magnitude_diagram) showing the red sequence (old galaxies, typically elliptical galaxies), the green valley (where the Milky Way is believed to be in), and the blue cloud (young galaxies, typically spiral galaxies)

The Milky Way began as one or several small overdensities in the mass distribution in the Universe shortly after the [Big Bang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Bang) 13.61 billion years ago.[^cite_note-PHYS-20170727-266][^cite_note-borah-267][^cite_note-legassick-268] Some of these overdensities were the seeds of globular clusters in which the oldest remaining stars in what is now the Milky Way formed. Nearly half the matter in the Milky Way may have come from other distant galaxies.[^cite_note-PHYS-20170727-266] These stars and clusters now comprise the stellar halo of the Milky Way. Within a few billion years of the birth of the first stars, the mass of the Milky Way was large enough that it was spinning relatively quickly. Due to [conservation of angular momentum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservation_of_angular_momentum), this led the gaseous interstellar medium to collapse from a roughly spheroidal shape to a disk. Therefore, later generations of stars formed in this spiral disk. Most younger stars, including the Sun, are observed to be in the disk.[^cite_note-ut20090527-269][^cite_note-Buser-270]

Since the first stars began to form, the Milky Way has grown through both [galaxy mergers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galaxy_merger) (particularly early in the Milky Way's growth) and accretion of gas directly from the Galactic halo.[^cite_note-Buser-270] The Milky Way is currently accreting material from several small galaxies, including two of its largest satellite galaxies, the [Large](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Large_Magellanic_Cloud) and [Small](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Small_Magellanic_Cloud) Magellanic Clouds, through the [Magellanic Stream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magellanic_Stream). Direct accretion of gas is observed in [high-velocity clouds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-velocity_cloud) like the [Smith Cloud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smith_Cloud).[^cite_note-araa35_217-271][^cite_note-apj679_L21-272]

Cosmological simulations indicate that, 11 billion years ago, it merged with a particularly large galaxy that has been labeled the [Kraken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kraken_galaxy).[^cite_note-273][^cite_note-274] Properties of the Milky Way such as stellar mass, [angular momentum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angular_momentum), and [metallicity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metallicity) in its outermost regions suggest it has undergone no mergers with large galaxies in the last 10 billion years. This lack of recent major mergers is unusual among spiral galaxies of a similar type.
